Post Newtonian emission of gravitational waves from binary systems: a gauge theory perspective
Francesco Fucito, and Jose Francisco Morales

Abstract
Using the AGT correspondence and localization, we derive a combinatorial formula for the Post-Newtonian expansion of the wave form describing the gravitational emission from binary systems made of objects of extremely different masses. The results are written as a double instanton series describing the expansion of the gravitational wave at large distances and small velocities, and are tested against previous formulae in the literature for Schwarschild and Kerr black holes at the 5th and 3rd Post Newtonian order respectively beyond the quadrupole approximation. Tidal effects show up in the wave form at the 5th PN order, providing a quantitative measure of sizes and reflectivity properties of the gravity solution.



1
Introduction

The detection of gravitational waves (GW) by the LIGO-Virgo collaboration
LIGOScientific:2016aoc
is definitively one of the most exciting achievements in contemporary physics.To extract the signal from the experimental data, templates of waveforms have been meticulously crafted
through a sophisticated blend of theoretical and numerical calculations simulating the three phases (inspiral, merging, ring down) of the coalescence of black holes (BH) and/or neutron stars. The matching against observations provides a strong test of general relativity and confirms the existence of black hole like objects in nature.
The upgrade of the LIGO-Virgo experiments and the coming of the ET experiment and the space based LISA will open a new era of precision gravitational wave (GW) astronomy allowing to test gravity beyond the realm of general relativity.

A fundamental question is to prove the existence of BH’s in nature. These mathematically beautiful solutions of the Einstein equations exhibit, at the classical level, remarkable uniqueness and stability properties. At the quantum level instead, a BH radiate and evaporates completely, burning all the information used to built it, in contradiction with the fundamental principle of unitarity in physics. Alternatives to black holes exist, and go under the collective name of Exotic Compact Objects (ECO). Examples are gravastars, wormholes, boson stars and fuzzballs
Mathur:2005zp
;
Cardoso:2016rao
;
Cardoso:2017cqb
;
Cardoso:2019rvt
. ECO’s are described by smooth horizonless geometries and can be distinguished from BH’s by their multipolar structure
Bena:2020see
;
Bianchi:2020bxa
;
Bena:2020uup
;
Bianchi:2020miz
;
Mayerson:2020tpn
;
Bah:2021jno
, quasi normal modes (QNM’s) and their characteristic echo based GW emission
Ikeda:2021uvc
. GW signals coming from a binary system can be described in analytic terms in the ringdown and inspiral phases. In

Aminov:2020yma
;
Bonelli:2021uvf
;
Bianchi:2021mft
;
Bonelli:2022ten
;
Bianchi:2022wku
;
Consoli:2022eey
;
Bianchi:2023sfs
;
Aminov:2023jve
, novel techniques
capitalizing on recent advances in quantum field theories have been exploited to study the QNM’s characterising the ringdown phase of the GW emission. More traditional analytical approaches to the dynamics of gravitationally interacting binary systems
Blanchet:2013haa
;
Damour:2016gwp
;
Damour:2017zjx
;
Bini:2023fiz
have been successfully compared against gauge inspired scattering amplitude techniques
Bjerrum-Bohr:2018xdl
;
Cheung:2018wkq
;
Bern:2019nnu
;
KoemansCollado:2019ggb
;
Bern:2019crd
;
Bjerrum-Bohr:2019kec
;
Bern:2020gjj
;
DiVecchia:2021ndb
;
DiVecchia:2021bdo
;
Cristofoli:2021vyo
;
Herrmann:2021tct
;
Brandhuber:2021eyq
;
Bjerrum-Bohr:2021vuf
;
Bjerrum-Bohr:2021din
;
Adamo:2022qci
;
Brandhuber:2023hhy
;
DiVecchia:2023frv
;
Brandhuber:2023hhl
;
DeAngelis:2023lvf
, effective field theories
Kalin:2020mvi
;
Kalin:2020fhe
;
Liu:2021zxr
;
Bjerrum-Bohr:2022ows
in the description of the inspiral phase. Other techniques like worldline quantum field theories
Mogull:2020sak
;
Jakobsen:2021smu
;
Jakobsen:2021lvp
;
Jakobsen:2023ndj
;
Jakobsen:2022psy
,
and two dimensional conformal field theories
Novaes:2014lha
;
CarneirodaCunha:2015hzd
;
CarneirodaCunha:2015qln
;
Amado:2017kao
;
Lencses:2017dgf
;
BarraganAmado:2018zpa
;
Novaes:2018fry
;
CarneirodaCunha:2019tia
;
Amado:2020zsr
;
BarraganAmado:2021uyw
;
Cavalcante:2021scq
;
daCunha:2021jkm

originally devised for different fields of research, were used for comparison with gravity computations.

In this paper we deal with the study of the inspiral phase of binary systems. We work in the extreme mass ratio limit of the inspiral motion (EMRI) where the light particle orbits for a long time around the heavy one accumulating thousands of radians bringing tidal heating in the sensitivity band of a space based interferometer like LISA
Hughes:2001jr
. In this regime the GW emission is well approximated by the the Post-Newtonian (PN) limit which assumes that the distance between the two objects is large and the relative velocity small. The theoretical studies of gravitational systems in the PN limit have a long history, and are very well described in many excellent textbooks
Straumann:1984xf
;
Maggiore:2007ulw
;
Maggiore:2018sht
;
PoissonWill:2014
. These results have been checked and further expanded using BH perturbation theory. In this framework, the Einstein equations are expanded to linear order in the perturbations around the background and written in terms of an ordinary second order differential equation of the Heun type resembling a Schrödinger equation.
A binary system can also be described along these lines introducing in the equations a source term. The PN results can now be checked against this independent computation. Early results appeared in
Poisson:1993vp
and were later expanded in a series of papers
Poisson:1993vp
;
Tagoshi:1993dm
;
Tagoshi:1994sm
;
Shibata:1994jx
;
Tanaka:1996lfd
;
Mino:1997bx
;
Fujita:2010xj
;
Fujita:2011zk
;
Fujita:2012cm
to extremely high orders for binary system orbiting circularly around Schwarzschild and Kerr BH’s.

We revisit the computation of the PN expansion of the waveform using the CFT/gravity correspondence developed in
Bonelli:2021uvf
;
Bonelli:2022ten
;
Consoli:2022eey
. This line of research is founded on the so called AGT correspondence
Alday:2009aq
which relates the results of localization for the partition functions of a
N=2
supersymmetric quiver gauge theories
Nekrasov:2002qd
;
Flume:2002az
;
Bruzzo:2002xf
;
Nekrasov:2003rj
with certain correlators of the Liouville CFT. The crucial point is that some correlators in the two-dimensional CFT satisfy a second order differential equation
Belavin:1984vu
which can be mapped to those of the Heun type which describe the wave form in the BH perturbation theory. As a result, the instanton gauge theory partition functions provide a combinatorial representation of the Heun function and its connection formulae
Bonelli:2022ten
;
Consoli:2022eey
. Following these ideas we relate
the PN description of the wave form in the near zone to the instanton partition function of a quiver gauge theory given as a double series in the couplings which are related to the PN expansion parameters. As a result the PN expansion is given as a series with coefficients given by rational functions of the orbital quantum number
\ell
. All logs and transcendental numbers that typically appear in the PN expansions are summed up into exponentials and Gamma functions. The instanton series is known to be convergent
Its:2014lga
;
Arnaudo:2022ivo
and as a consequence so is the PN expansion.

Tidal effects are expected to show up in the gravitational wave signal at the 5th PN order (
v^{10}
) when the two independent solutions of the Heun equation start to mix
Bildsten:1992my
;
Tanaka:1996lfd
. Their effects
are proportional to the dynamical Love number defined in
Charalambous:2021mea
;
Consoli:2022eey
and provide a quantitative measure of the compactness of the gravitational object. Along these lines we compute the static and dynamic Love numbers of Kerr-like compact objects. Recent results on this subject appeared recently in
Maggio:2021uge
.

This is the plan of the paper: In Section 2 we introduce the binary system and review the derivation of the wave form and luminosity
of the gravitational wave signal. In section 3, we describe the CFT gravity dictionary adapting previous results
Consoli:2022eey
to the needs of the present discussion. In Section 4 and 5 we derive the PN expansion of the the wave form and its luminosity for Schwarschild and Kerr BH’s at the 5th and 3rd PN order beyond the quadrupole approximation. In Section 6 we discuss the tidal interactions and point out the differences between BH’s and ECO’s.
Appendices
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
collect some background and technical material.

2
The binary system

In this section we briefly review some background material about binary systems following
Tagoshi:1993dm
;
Tagoshi:1994sm
;
Shibata:1994jx
;
Tanaka:1996lfd
;
Mino:1997bx
;
Fujita:2010xj
;
Fujita:2011zk
;
Fujita:2012cm
whose notations we follow. We use units such that
c=G=1
and adopt a mostly plus Lorentzian metric.

2.1
Teukolsky equations

The dynamics of the linear perturbations of the Kerr metric

ds^{2}=\frac{\Delta_{r}(r)}{\Sigma}(dt-a\sin^{2}\theta d\phi)^{2}+\frac{\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\left[(r^{2}+a^{2})d\phi-a\,dt\right]^{2}+\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta_{r}(r)}dr^{2}+\Sigma d\theta^{2}

(1)

with
\Delta_{r}(r)=(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-})=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2},\,\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta

can be decomposed into spin
s
components,
\Psi_{s}
, obtained as projections
of the curvature tensors deviation from the Kerr metric. The Einstein equations for
\Psi_{s}
can be separated into radial and angular components via the ansatz

\displaystyle\Psi_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\omega\,\sum_{\ell,m}e^{{\rm i}m\phi-{\rm i}\omega t}R_{\ell m}(r){}_{s}S_{\ell m}(\chi)

(2)

with
\chi=\cos\theta
, and the functions
R_{\ell m}(r)
,
{}_{s}S_{\ell m}(\chi)
satisfying the Teukolsky equations
Teukolsky:1972my

\displaystyle{d\over d\chi}\left[\Delta_{\chi}(\chi){}_{s}S_{\ell m}^{\prime}(\chi)\right]+\left(-\frac{(m+s\chi)^{2}}{1-\chi^{2}}+s+A+a^{2}\chi^{2}\omega^{2}-2as\chi\omega\right){}_{s}S_{\ell m}(\chi)=0

(3)

\displaystyle{1\over\Delta_{r}(r)^{s}}{d\over dr}\left[\Delta_{r}(r)^{s+1}R_{\ell m}^{\prime}(r)\right]{+}\left(\frac{K(r)^{2}{-}2{\rm i}s(r{-}M)K(r)}{\Delta_{r}(r)}{+}4{\rm i}s\omega r{-}\lambda_{r}\right)R_{\ell m}(r)={}_{s}T_{\ell m}(r)

Here
M
is the mass,
a=J/M
, the
angular momentum parameter,
A=(\ell-s)(\ell+s+1)+O(\omega a)
is a separation constant and

\Delta_{\chi}(\chi)=1-\chi^{2},\quad\lambda_{r}=A+a^{2}\omega^{2}-2am\omega,\quad K(r)=(r^{2}+a^{2})\omega-am

(4)

Finally

{}_{s}T_{\ell m}(r)={1\over\sqrt{2\pi}}\int d\chi\,d\phi\,dt\,{}_{s}S_{\ell m}(\chi)\,e^{-im\phi+i\omega t}\Sigma(r,\chi)\,{}_{s}{\cal T}({\bf x})

(5)

is the Fourier transform of the spin
s
stress-energy tensor
{}_{s}{\cal T}({\bf x})
. We refer the reader to
Teukolsky:1972my
for details. We are interested in the spin
s=-2
mode associated to the
\psi_{4}
-perturbation.
The explicit form of
{}_{s}T_{\ell m}
has been derived in

Tagoshi:1993dm
;
Tagoshi:1994sm
;
Shibata:1994jx
;
Tanaka:1996lfd
;
Mino:1997bx
;
Fujita:2010xj
;
Fujita:2011zk
;
Fujita:2012cm
. For the reader’s convenience, we collect in Appendix
B
, the formulae relevant to the computations in this paper.

2.2
The Green function

The radial Teukolsky equation with a non-trivial source
{}_{s}T_{\ell m}(r)
can be solved with the method of the Green function.
For simplicity, we shall omit in the following the subscripts
\ell,m
. Given
\ell,m
, we denote by
T
the source term, by
R_{T}
the solution of the inhomogenous equation with incoming boundary conditions at the horizon and by
R_{\rm in,up}
the solutions of the homogenous equation, satisfying incoming boundary conditions at the horizon and outgoing ones at infinity respectively. The Green function will be written in terms of
R_{\rm in}
and
R_{\rm up}
.
More explicitly, we write

R_{T}(r)=\int_{r_{+}}^{\infty}G(r,r^{\prime})\,{}_{-2}T(r^{\prime})\Delta_{r}(r^{\prime})^{-2}dr^{\prime}.

(6)

with
G(r,r^{\prime})
the Green function with incoming boundary conditions at the horizon. Explicitly

G(r,r^{\prime})={1\over W}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}R_{\rm in}(r)R_{\rm up}(r^{\prime})&&r<r^{\prime}\\
R_{\rm in}(r^{\prime})R_{\rm up}(r)&&r>r^{\prime}\\
\end{array}\right.

(7)

with
R_{\rm in/up}
satisfying the boundary conditions

\displaystyle R_{\rm in}(r)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}D_{\rm in}\Delta_{r}(r)^{2}e^{-{\rm i}kr_{*}}&&\quad r\to r_{+}\\
B_{\rm up}r^{3}e^{{\rm i}\omega r_{*}}+B_{\rm in}r^{-1}e^{-{\rm i}\omega r_{*}}&&\quad r\to\infty\\
\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle R_{\rm up}(r)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\widetilde{D}_{\rm up}\,e^{{\rm i}kr_{*}}+\widetilde{D}_{\rm in}\,\Delta_{r}(r)^{2}e^{-{\rm i}kr_{*}}&&r\to r_{+}\\
\widetilde{B}_{\rm up}r^{3}e^{{\rm i}\omega r_{*}}&&r\to\infty\\
\end{array}\right.

where
k=\omega-ma/2Mr_{+}
,
r_{*}=r+\frac{2Mr_{+}}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\log\frac{r-r_{+}}{2M}-\frac{2Mr_{-}}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\log\frac{r-r_{-}}{2M}
is the tortoise coordinate and
W
the Wronskian

W=\left[R_{\rm in}(r)R_{\rm up}^{\prime}(r)-R_{\rm in}^{\prime}(r)R_{\rm up}(r)\right]/\Delta_{r}(r)=2{\rm i}\omega\,B_{\rm in}\widetilde{B}_{\rm up}

(14)

Here we use the fact that
W
is a constant, as follows from the wave equation, so it has been evaluated at
r\to\infty
.
Putting altogether one finds

R_{T}(r)\underset{r\to\infty}{\approx}r^{3}e^{{\rm i}\omega r_{*}}Z_{\ell m}

(15)

with

Z_{\ell m}=\int_{r_{+}}^{\infty}{R_{\rm in}(r^{\prime})\over 2{\rm i}\omega B_{\rm in}}{}_{-2}T(r^{\prime})\Delta_{r}(r^{\prime})^{-2}dr^{\prime}

(16)

2.3
Circular orbits

The stress energy tensor produced by the particle motion is given by

T^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\int d\tau\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}\delta^{(4)}\left[x-x_{0}(\tau)\right]

(17)

with
x_{0}(\tau)
the geodesic trajectory. In the Hamiltonian formalism, the dynamics of a particle moving along a geodetics of the Kerr geometry is governed by the separable Hamiltonian

{\mathcal{H}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(g^{\mu\nu}P_{\mu}P_{\nu}+\mu^{2})={\mathcal{H}}_{r}+{\mathcal{H}}_{\theta}=0

(18)

with
\mu
the particle mass,

\displaystyle 2\Sigma\mathcal{H}_{r}

\displaystyle=P_{r}^{2}{\Delta_{r}}-\frac{\left(P_{t}(a^{2}+r^{2})+a{P_{\phi}}\right)^{2}}{{\Delta_{r}}}+(aP_{t}+P_{\phi})^{2}-\mu^{2}r^{2}-C=0

(19)

\displaystyle 2\Sigma\mathcal{H}_{\theta}

\displaystyle=P_{\theta}^{2}+P_{\phi}^{2}\cot^{2}\theta-P_{t}^{2}a^{2}\cos\theta^{2}-\mu^{2}a^{2}\cos\theta^{2}+C=0

and
C
a separation constant. We are interested in circular geodesics along the equatorial plane
\theta=\pi/2
.
They are defined by the solutions of

P_{\theta}(\theta)=P_{r}(r)=\partial_{r}P_{r}(r)=0

(20)

with
P_{\theta}(\theta)
,
P_{r}(r)
obtained by solving (
19
).
It is convenient to introduce the parameters
v
,
\mathfrak{q}
to parametrize the radius
r_{0}
of the orbit and the angular parameter
a
via the identifications

r_{0}=Mv^{-2}\quad,\quad a=\mathfrak{q}M

(21)

In terms of these variables the solutions of (
19
) and (
20
)
are

P_{t}=-\mu{1-2v^{2}+\mathfrak{q}v^{3}\over\sqrt{1-3v^{2}+2\mathfrak{q}v^{3}}}\quad,\quad P_{\phi}={\mu M\over v}{(1-2\mathfrak{q}v^{3}+\mathfrak{q}^{2}v^{4})\over\sqrt{1-3v^{2}+2\mathfrak{q}v^{3}}}\quad,\quad C=0

(22)

The geodesic orbit is determined by integrating the velocities

\dot{x}^{\mu}={dx^{\mu}\over d\tau}={\partial{\cal H}\over\partial P_{\mu}}

(23)

Explicitly

\dot{t}=\frac{\mu v^{3}}{M\sqrt{1-3v^{2}+2\mathfrak{q}v^{3}}}\qquad,\qquad\dot{\phi}=\frac{\mu\left(\mathfrak{q}v^{3}+1\right)}{\sqrt{1-3v^{2}+2\mathfrak{q}v^{3}}}\quad,\quad\dot{r}=\dot{\theta}=0

(24)

The angular velocity is therefore

\Omega={d\phi\over dt}={\dot{\phi}\over\dot{t}}={v^{3}\over M(1+\mathfrak{q}v^{3})}

(25)

2.4
Wave form and energy flux

Putting together (
17
), (
5
) and (
16
), one finds
Mino:1997bx

Z_{\ell m}={{\pi}\over{{\rm i}\omega B_{{\rm in}}}}\left[A_{0}R_{{\rm in}}(r_{0}){+}A_{1}R^{\prime}_{{\rm in}}(r_{0}){+}A_{2}R^{\prime\prime}_{{\rm in}}(r_{0})\right]_{\omega=m\Omega}

(26)

with the
A_{i}
’s given by (
136
) in Appendix
B
.
In terms of
Z_{\ell m}
, the wave form takes the form

h_{+}-{\rm i}h_{\times}={2\pi\over r}\sum_{\ell,m}{Z_{\ell m}\over\omega^{2}}{}_{-2}S_{\ell m}(\chi)e^{{\rm i}\omega(r^{*}-t)+{\rm i}m\phi}

(27)

and the gravitational luminosity (the amount of energy carried away per unit time) is given by

{dE\over dt}=\mu^{2}\sum_{\ell,m}{|Z_{\ell,m}|^{2}\over 4\pi\omega^{2}}

(28)

In the next section we will derive a PN expansion of these formulae for the Schwarzschild and Kerr geometries.

3
CFT gravity correspondence

The basic idea behind the CFT gravity correspondence
Bonelli:2021uvf
;
Bonelli:2022ten
;
Consoli:2022eey
relies on the fact, that
the type of differential equations governing the dynamics of black hole perturbations appear in the context of Liouville theory as conformal Ward identities
Belavin:1984vu
, satisfied by certain five points conformal blocks in the limit of large central charge.

According to the AGT correspondence, the degenerate conformal block is in turn related to the instanton partition function of a
N=2
supersymmetric quiver gauge theory
Fucito:2004gi
;
Alday:2009aq
with gauge group
SU(2)^{2}
. The large central charge limit corresponds to the Nekrasov-Shatashvili limit
Nekrasov:2009rc
of the instanton partition function. In this section we collect the formulae
Bonelli:2022ten
;
Consoli:2022eey
which will be relevent to our computation. We refer the reader to these references and Appendix
A
for details.

3.1
Confluent Heun equation

The homogeneous part of the Teukolsky angular and radial equations (
3
) can be both brought into the normal form

\left[{d^{2}\over dz^{2}}+Q(z)\right]\Psi(z)=0

(29)

with
z
expressed in terms of the angular or radial variable and
Consoli:2022eey

Q(z)=-\frac{{x}^{2}}{4z^{4}}+\frac{{x}\,c}{z^{3}}+\frac{u-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}+x({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}-k_{0})}{(z-1)z^{2}}+\frac{{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}-k_{0}^{2}}{(z-1)^{2}z}+\frac{{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}-p_{0}^{2}}{(z-1)z}

(30)

(
30
) has two regular singularities at
z=\infty,1
and an irregular one at
z=0
. The solution can be written in terms of confluent Heun functions with parameters expressed in terms of
p_{0}
,
k_{0}
,
c
,
u
and
x
.
The parametrization (
30
) is inspired by the correspondence with a five-point correlator in the Liouville theory that will be discussed below.

The angular equation

The angular equation can be written in the normal form (
29
), by writing

\chi={2\over z}-1\qquad,\qquad{}_{s}S_{\ell m}={\Psi(z)\over\sqrt{1-\chi^{2}}}

(31)

and identifying
1
1

1

The superscript
\chi
is to distinguish this angular equation from the radial one.

x^{\chi}=4a\omega\penalty\ ,\penalty\ c^{\chi}=s\penalty\ ,\penalty\ p_{0}^{\chi}=\frac{s-m}{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ k^{\chi}_{0}=\frac{s+m}{2}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ A=u^{\chi}-(s+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})^{2}+2a\omega(1-m)-a^{2}\omega^{2}

(32)

The radial equation

The homogenous part of the radial equation can be written in the normal form (
29
), by taking

z={r_{+}-r_{-}\over r-r_{-}}\qquad,\qquad R_{\rm in}=(1-z)^{-{s+1\over 2}}\,z^{s}\,\Psi(z)

(33)

and identifying

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4i\delta\omega\qquad,\qquad c=s{-}2iM\omega\qquad,\qquad k_{0}={\textstyle\frac{s}{2}}-iP_{+}\qquad,\qquad p_{0}={\textstyle\frac{s}{2}}+iP_{-}

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A{+}(s{+}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})^{2}{+}2\omega(am{-}i\delta){+}\omega^{2}(a^{2}{-}12M^{2}{-}8M\delta)

(34)

with

\displaystyle r_{\pm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M\pm\delta=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}\qquad,\qquad P_{\pm}=\frac{am{-}2M\omega r_{\pm}}{r_{+}-r_{-}}

(35)

3.2
The five-point conformal block

We consider the five point conformal blocks,
\Psi_{\pm}(z)
, with the insertion of a degenerate field at position
z
, two primaries at
\infty
,
1
, and two colliding at
0
.
\Psi_{\pm}(z)
are the independent solutions of (
29
)
2
2

2

In the paper
\pm
and
\pm 1
are interchangeable.
and are functions of the momenta,
p_{0}
,
k_{0}
,
c
, of the primary fields and are distinguished by the field exchanged in the intermediate channel. Using the AGT correspondence they can be written as

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha}(z)=\lim_{b\to 0}\,e^{{x\over 2z}}\,z^{{1\over 2}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}\,\left(1{-}z\right)^{(2k_{0}{-}1{-}b^{2})(2k_{\rm deg}{+}1{+}b^{2})\over 2b^{2}}{Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha}}{}^{k_{\rm deg}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}}{}_{c}(z,{\textstyle\frac{x}{z}})\over Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}}{}_{c}(x)}

(36)

with
Flume:2002az
;
Bruzzo:2002xf
;
Fucito:2004gi
;
Alday:2009aq

\displaystyle Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}c}(q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{W}q^{|W|}\frac{z^{\rm bifund}_{\emptyset,W}(p_{0},\mathfrak{a},-k_{0})z^{\rm hyp}_{W}(\mathfrak{a},-c)}{z^{\rm bifund}_{W,W}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a},{\textstyle\frac{b^{2}+1}{2}})}

(37)

\displaystyle Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha}}{}^{k_{1}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}c}(q_{1},q_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{Y,W}q_{1}^{|Y|}q_{2}^{|W|}\frac{z^{\rm bifund}_{\emptyset,Y}(p_{0},\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha},-k_{0})z^{\rm bifund}_{Y,W}(p_{1},\mathfrak{a},-k_{1})z^{\rm hyp}_{W}(\mathfrak{a},-c)}{z^{\rm bifund}_{Y,Y}(\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha},\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha},{\textstyle\frac{b^{2}+1}{2}})z^{\rm bifund}_{W,W}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{a},{\textstyle\frac{b^{2}+1}{2}})}

(38)

the instanton partition functions of
SU(2)
and
SU(2)^{2}
gauge theories with three flavours. The sums in (
38
) run over the pairs of Young tableau
\{Y_{\pm}\}
,
\{W_{\pm}\}
while
|Y|
,
|W|
denote the total number boxes in each pair.
The functions
z^{\rm bifund}_{Y,W}
,
z^{\rm hyp}_{Y_{2}}
represent the contributions of a hypermultiplet transforming in the bifundamental and fundamental representation of the gauge group and are given by a product over the boxes of the Young tableau specified by
s=(i,j)
(see Appendix
A
for details)

\displaystyle z^{\rm bifund}_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}(p,p^{\prime},m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}\prod_{s\in\Lambda_{\beta}}\left[E_{\Lambda_{\beta},\Lambda^{\prime}_{\beta^{\prime}}}(\beta p{-}\beta^{\prime}p^{\prime},s){-}m\right]\prod_{t\in\Lambda^{\prime}_{\beta^{\prime}}}\left[{-}E_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{\beta^{\prime}},\Lambda_{\beta}}(\beta^{\prime}p^{\prime}{-}\beta p,t){-}m\right]

\displaystyle z^{\rm hyp}_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{a},m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\beta}\prod_{s\in\Lambda_{\beta}}\left[-E_{\Lambda_{\beta},\emptyset}(\beta\mathfrak{a},s)+m\right]

(39)

with

\displaystyle E_{\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}}(x,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x-(\lambda_{\Lambda^{\prime}j}^{T}-i)+b^{2}(\lambda_{\Lambda i}-j+1)-{\textstyle\frac{b^{2}+1}{2}}

(40)

where
\lambda_{\Lambda i}
is the number of boxes in the
i
-th row and
\lambda_{\Lambda j}^{T}
is the number of boxes in the
j
-th column of the tableau
\Lambda
.
The conformal field theory variables entering in the five-point conformal block are related to the
SU(2)^{2}
gauge theory couplings,
\Omega
-background parameters and masses via the dictionary

\displaystyle q_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z\quad,\quad q_{2}={x\over z}\quad,\quad\epsilon_{1}=1\quad,\quad\epsilon_{2}=b^{2}

\displaystyle m_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{0}+p_{0}\quad,\quad m_{2}=k_{0}-p_{0}\quad,\quad m_{3}=-c\quad,\quad m_{\rm bifund}=-k_{\rm deg}

(41)

Similar relations hold between the four-point conformal block and the
SU(2)
gauge theory with coupling now given by
q=q_{1}q_{2}=x
.
On the other hand

\displaystyle k_{\rm deg}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}+b^{2}\quad,\quad p_{s}^{\alpha}=p_{s}+\frac{\alpha b^{2}}{2}

(42)

are the momenta of the degenerate insertion and of its OPE with a primary field with momentum
p_{s}
Belavin:1984vu
. We notice that each factor in (
36
) is divergent in the limit
b\to 0
but the product is finite. The result is given by a double series expansion

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z^{{1\over 2}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}\left[1+z\frac{k_{0}^{2}-p_{0}^{2}+\mathfrak{a}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}}{1+2\alpha\mathfrak{a}}-\frac{c{x}}{z\left(1-2\alpha\mathfrak{a}\right)}\right.

(43)

\displaystyle\left.-c{x}\left(\frac{4\left(k_{0}^{2}-p_{0}^{2}\right)\left(2\alpha\mathfrak{a}+3\right)-(3-2\alpha\mathfrak{a})(1+2\alpha\mathfrak{a})^{2}}{4\left(1-2\alpha\mathfrak{a}\right)\left(2\alpha\mathfrak{a}+1\right){}^{2}}\right)\right]+\ldots

Finally the parameter
\mathfrak{a}
labels the momenta of the Liouville field exchanged in the intermediate channel and characterizes the monodromy of the gravity solutions around
z=0

\Psi_{\alpha}\left(e^{2\pi{\rm i}}z\right)=e^{2\pi{\rm i}\left({1\over 2}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}\right)}\Psi_{\alpha}\left(z\right)

(44)

In the gauge theory framework
\mathfrak{a}
represents the scalar vacuum expectation value (the fundamental Seiberg-Witten period) and can be determined by solving

u=\mathfrak{a}^{2}-{x}\partial_{x}{\cal F}_{\rm inst}(\mathfrak{a})

(45)

as a series in
x
with

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{\rm inst}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to 0}b^{2}\ln\left[Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}}{}_{c}(x)\right]=\frac{x}{2}\left[(1+c-2k_{0})-{4c\left(k_{0}^{2}-p_{0}^{2}\right)\over 1-4\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\right]+\ldots

(46)

The result reads

\mathfrak{a}=\sqrt{u}+{x\over\sqrt{u}}\left[{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\left(1+c-2k_{0}\right)+\frac{c\left(k_{0}^{2}-p_{0}^{2}\right)}{4u-1}\right]+\ldots

(47)

3.3
Braiding and fusion connection formulae

According to the gauge gravity correspondence, the double instanton series (
43
) describes the PN expansion of the gravitational waveform in the limit where both
z
and
x/z
are small. In the gravity variables, see (
33
-
34
), this corresponds to the region where the distance from horizons are large but still much smaller than the wavelength of the perturbation

\omega(r-r_{-})\ll 1\qquad{\rm and}\qquad r\gg r_{+}

(48)

We will refer to this region as the
near zone
. We notice that what it is typically called the near zone is the region where
\omega(r-r_{+})\ll 1
.
The PN expansion requires in addition that
r\gg r_{+}
, so we could better call the region described by (
48
), the asymptotically far near zone, but for simplicity we will refer to it as the near zone.
Moreover, we will further divide this zone into two patches depending on whether
x/z\ll z
(int) or
z\ll x/z
(ext). In these two extreme cases the instanton series (
43
) can be resummed and written in terms of the hypergeometric functions

\displaystyle\widehat{\widehat{\psi}}_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\lim_{{\textstyle\frac{x}{z}}\to 0}\Psi_{\alpha}(z)=z^{{1\over 2}{+}\alpha\mathfrak{a}}(1{-}z)^{{1\over 2}{+}k_{0}}{}_{2}F_{1}({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{+}k_{0}{+}p_{0}{+}\alpha\mathfrak{a},{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{+}k_{0}{-}p_{0}+\alpha\mathfrak{a},1{+}2\alpha\mathfrak{a},z)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\psi}_{\alpha}({\textstyle\frac{x}{z}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle x^{-{1\over 2}-\alpha\mathfrak{a}}\lim_{z\to 0}\Psi_{\alpha}(z)=\left({\textstyle\frac{z}{x}}\right)^{{1\over 2}{+}\alpha\mathfrak{a}}e^{-{x\over 2z}}{}_{1}F_{1}({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}-c-\alpha\mathfrak{a},1-2\alpha\mathfrak{a},{\textstyle\frac{x}{z}})

(49)

In these patches the effective potential can be approximated as

\displaystyle Q_{\rm int}(z)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{a}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}}{(z-1)z^{2}}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-k_{0}^{2}}{(z-1)^{2}z}+\frac{\frac{1}{4}-p_{0}^{2}}{(z-1)z}

\displaystyle Q_{\rm ext}(z)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-\frac{{x}^{2}}{4z^{4}}+\frac{{x}\,c}{z^{3}}+\frac{\mathfrak{a}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}}{(z-1)z^{2}}

(50)

Using the standard hypergeometric connection formulae these functions can be alternatively written as

\displaystyle\widehat{\widehat{\psi}}_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\widehat{\psi}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(z)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\psi}_{\alpha}({\textstyle\frac{x}{z}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha^{\prime}}({\textstyle\frac{x}{z}})

(51)

with

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(z)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle z^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathfrak{a}}\,(1{-}z)^{\frac{1}{2}{+}\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{+}\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}{+}p_{0}{+}\mathfrak{a},\frac{1}{2}{+}\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}{-}p_{0}{+}\mathfrak{a};1{+}2\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0};1{-}z\right)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha^{\prime}}({\textstyle\frac{z}{x}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left({\textstyle\frac{z}{x}}\right)^{1+\alpha^{\prime}c}e^{{\alpha^{\prime}x\over 2z}}\,_{2}F_{0}\left({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{+}\alpha^{\prime}c{+}\mathfrak{a};{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{+}\alpha^{\prime}c{-}\mathfrak{a};{\textstyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}z}{x}}\right)

(52)

and

\displaystyle F_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(1+2\alpha\mathfrak{a})\Gamma\left(-2\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+p_{0}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-p_{0}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}-\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}\right)}

\displaystyle F^{-1}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(1+2\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0})\Gamma\left(-2\alpha\mathfrak{a}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}+p_{0}-\alpha\mathfrak{a}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}k_{0}-p_{0}-\alpha\mathfrak{a}\right)}

\displaystyle B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\prime})^{-{1\over 2}+\alpha p_{2}+\alpha^{\prime}c}\frac{\Gamma\left(1-2\alpha\mathfrak{a}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\alpha\mathfrak{a}-\alpha^{\prime}c\right)}

(53)

\widehat{\psi}_{\alpha}
,
\widetilde{\psi}_{\alpha}
arise as limits of the conformal blocks

\widehat{\Psi}_{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\alpha}
describing the
near horizon
and
far zone
respectively.
The latters correspond to different OPE expansion of the same five-point correlator and are related to each other by the CFT braiding and fusion relations

Bonelli:2021uvf
;
Bonelli:2022ten
;
Consoli:2022eey

\widehat{\Psi}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(z)=F^{-1}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\alpha}\Psi_{\alpha}(z)\qquad,\qquad\Psi_{\alpha}(z)=B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\tilde{\Psi}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)

(54)

where the fusion and braiding matrices are given in (
53
).
Any solution of the homogenous differential equation can be written in the alternative ways

\Psi(z)=\sum_{\alpha=\pm}c_{\alpha}\widehat{\Psi}_{\alpha}(z)=\sum_{\alpha=\pm}\hat{c}_{\alpha}\widehat{\Psi}_{\alpha}(z)=\sum_{\alpha=\pm}\tilde{c}_{\alpha}\widetilde{\Psi}_{\alpha}(z)

(55)

with

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=F_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,c_{\alpha}\qquad,\qquad\tilde{c}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=c_{\alpha}\,x^{{1\over 2}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}

(56)

For example, the solution
\Psi_{\rm in}(z)
satisfying the incoming boundary conditions

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rm in}(z)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}(1-z)^{{1\over 2}-k_{0}}&&\qquad\quad z\to 1\\
C_{-}e^{-\frac{{x}}{2z}}z^{1-c}+C_{+}e^{\frac{{x}}{2z}}z^{1+c}&&\qquad\quad z\to 0\\
\end{array}\right.

can be written in the three regions as

\Psi_{\rm in}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllll}\widehat{\Psi}_{-}(z)&&{\rm Near\penalty\ horizon}&&z\approx 1\\
F^{-1}_{-,\alpha}\Psi_{\alpha}(z)&&{\rm Near\penalty\ zone}&&1\gg z\gg x\\
x^{{1\over 2}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}F^{-1}_{-,\alpha}B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\widetilde{\Psi}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)&&{\rm Far\penalty\ away}&&1\gg x\gg z\end{array}\right.

(60)

For the PN expansion we will use

\Psi_{\rm in}=\sum_{\alpha}F^{-1}_{-,\alpha}\Psi_{\alpha}

(61)

On the other hand, the asymptotics at infinity is obtained by expanding the conformal blocks
\widetilde{\Psi}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(z)
near
z=0
.
In this limit, the five-point conformal block factorizes into a four-point function depending only on
x
,
times a function of
z
(see Appendix
A
for details). The latter is obtained by expanding the hypergeometric function in the second line of (
52
) in the limit where
z\ll x
. One finds

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rm in}(z)\underset{z\to 0}{\approx}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}F^{-1}_{-,\alpha}B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,e^{{\alpha^{\prime}{x}\over 2z}}z^{1+\alpha^{\prime}c}x^{-{1\over 2}+\alpha^{\prime}c+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}\lim_{b\to 0}{Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha}c^{-\alpha^{\prime}}}(-x)\over Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}c}(-x)}=

(62)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}F^{-1}_{-,\alpha}B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,e^{{\alpha^{\prime}{x}\over 2z}}z^{1+\alpha^{\prime}c}\,x^{-{1\over 2}-\alpha^{\prime}c+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime}\partial_{c}+\alpha\partial_{\mathfrak{a}}){\cal F}_{\rm inst}(-x)}

Comparing against (

C_{\alpha^{\prime}}({x})=\sum_{\alpha}F^{-1}_{-,\alpha}B^{\rm conf}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,e^{{\alpha^{\prime}{x}\over 2z}}\,x^{-{1\over 2}-\alpha^{\prime}c+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime}\partial_{c}+\alpha\partial_{\mathfrak{a}}){\cal F}_{\rm inst}(-x)}

4
Schwarzschild BH

In this section, we deal with the case of the Schwarzschild geometry. We consider a particle of mass
\mu
moving in a circular orbit of radius
r_{0}
along the equatorial plane.
From (
23
) and (

\dot{x}^{\mu}=\left(-{P_{t}\,r_{0}^{2}\over\Delta(r_{0})},0,0,{P_{\phi}\over r_{0}^{2}}\right)={\mu\over\sqrt{1-3v^{2}}}\left(1,0,0,\Omega\right)

(64)

with

v^{2}=\frac{M}{r_{0}}\qquad,\qquad\Omega={v^{3}\over M}

(65)

For circular orbits

\omega=m\Omega={mv^{3}\over M}

(66)

4.1
CFT gravity dictionary

In the Schwarschild case the angular equation can be solved in terms of the spin weighted
spheroidal harmonics
{}_{-2}S_{\ell m}(\cos\theta)
with eigenvalues

A=(\ell+2)(\ell-1)

(67)

The radial equation reduces to

with

\displaystyle\Delta_{r}(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(r-2M)

(69)

As we said earlier the radial equation can be put into the normal form (
29

and

Comparing against (

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4i\omega M\qquad,\qquad z={2M\over r}

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{-}2{-}2iM\omega\quad,\quad p_{0}=-1\quad,\quad k_{0}={-}1{+}2iM\omega\quad,\quad u=(\ell{+}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})^{2}{-}2iM\omega{-}20\omega^{2}M^{2}

4.2
The PN expansion of

and

We notice that the PN expansion is largely dominated by the

computing the ratio response/source. We will study this function later in this paper. Here we just notice that the leading contribution for a Schwarschild BH’s vanishes in the static limit
\omega\to 0

3

In the case of the Kerr geometry both the radial and the angular equations are of the confluent Heun type and the separation constant

6
Tidal interactions

Tidal interactions describe the response of a gravitational object to small perturbations of the geometry. They show up in the PN expansion of the wave form at order
v^{10}
, where the solutions
\Psi_{\pm}
describing the propagation of the wave in the near zone start to mix.
The strength of the tidal interactions is measured by the Love and dissipation numbers defined as the real and imaginary parts of the ratio
between the coefficients of the source and response components
\Psi_{\pm}
of the solution in the near zone. This ratio is sensitive to the boundary conditions defining
\Psi(z)
and provide therefore a quantitative measure of the compactness properties of the geometry.

In this section, following
Consoli:2022eey
, we compute the dynamical Love and dissipation numbers for Kerr BH’s and Kerr like compact objects. A spin s perturbation of a Kerr BH is described by a solution of the confluent Heun equation with incoming boundary conditions
\Psi=\widehat{\Psi}_{-}
leading to

\displaystyle R_{\rm Kerr}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-z)^{-{(s+1)\over 2}}\,z^{s}\,\sum_{\alpha=\pm}F^{-1}_{-\alpha}\Psi_{\alpha}(z)

(102)

with

\Psi_{\alpha}(z)\underset{z,{\textstyle\frac{\omega}{z}}\to 0}{\approx}z^{{1\over 2}+\mathfrak{a}}(1+\ldots)

(103)

The dynamical Love and dissipation numbers are given as the real and imaginary parts of the ratio of the
\Psi_{\pm}
coefficients

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm Kerr}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{F^{-1}_{-+}\over F^{-1}_{--}}=\frac{\Gamma(-2\mathfrak{a})\Gamma({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}+m_{1}+\mathfrak{a})\Gamma({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}+m_{2}+\mathfrak{a})}{\Gamma(2\mathfrak{a})\Gamma({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}+m_{1}-\mathfrak{a})\Gamma({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}+m_{2}-\mathfrak{a})}

(104)

with

\displaystyle m_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{0}+p_{0}=s+{\rm i}\epsilon\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptm_{2}=k_{0}-p_{0}=-2{\rm i}P_{+}-{\rm i}\epsilon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\ell}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}=\ell+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}-\epsilon^{2}\frac{\left(15\ell^{4}+30\ell^{3}+28\ell^{2}+13\ell+24\right)}{2\ell(\ell+1)(2\ell-1)(2\ell+1)(2\ell+3)}+O(\epsilon^{4})

(105)

Here we introduced the notion of “quantum” orbital number
\widehat{\ell}(\omega)
, that account for quantum corrections of the SW period

\mathfrak{a}(\epsilon)
. The non-trivial
\omega
-dependence of the tidal response function
{\cal L}_{\rm Kerr}
is encoded in the replacement
\ell\to\widehat{\ell}
.

The analysis above can be easily adapted to the case of a compact Kerr like geometry. This geometry is obtained
by cutting out a ball of radius
r_{+}+\Delta r
around the origin and replacing
the internal region by a horizonless geometry. Since the metric in the external region is that of Kerr, the solution outside is given again by a linear combination of
\Psi_{\alpha}
. The precise combination depends on the reflectivity assumed at the boundary of the two regions.
For simplicity, we assume here that
\Delta r\ll r_{+}
, so boundary conditions are imposed at
r=r_{+}
, where now a partial reflection of the wave is allowed. We write

\Psi=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}D_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\widehat{\Psi}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(z)=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime\prime}}D_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}F^{-1}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\alpha}\Psi_{\alpha}(z)

(106)

with

{\cal R}={D_{+}\over D_{-}}

(107)

a number, parametrizing the reflectivity of the boundary. Expanding the right hand side of (
106
) in the near zone, one finds

R_{\rm ECO}(z)\sim\Psi_{-}(z)+{\cal L}_{\rm ECO}\Psi_{+}(z)\sim\left[z^{s-\widehat{\ell}}(1+\ldots)+z^{s+\widehat{\ell}+1}\,{\cal L}_{\rm ECO}(1+\ldots)\right]

(108)

with

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{ECO}={\sum_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}D_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}F^{-1}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}+}\over\sum_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}D_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}F^{-1}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}-}}={{\cal L}_{\rm Kerr}+{\cal R}\gamma_{+}\over 1+{\cal R}\gamma_{-}}

(109)

and

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{F^{-1}_{++}\over F^{-1}_{--}}=\frac{\Gamma({-}2\widehat{\ell}{-}1)\Gamma(\widehat{\ell}{+}1{+}s{+}i\epsilon)\Gamma(1{-}s{+}2{\rm i}P_{+})\Gamma(\widehat{\ell}{+}1{-}2{\rm i}P_{+}{-}{\rm i}\epsilon)}{\Gamma(2\widehat{\ell}{+}1)\Gamma\left({-}\widehat{\ell}{-}s{-}{\rm i}\epsilon\right)\Gamma\left(1{+}s{-}2{\rm i}P_{+}\right)\Gamma({-}\widehat{\ell}{+}2{\rm i}P_{+}{+}i\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{F^{-1}_{+-}\over F^{-1}_{--}}=\frac{\Gamma(\widehat{\ell}{+}1{+}s{+}i\epsilon)\Gamma(1{-}s{+}2{\rm i}P_{+})\Gamma(\widehat{\ell}{+}1{-}2{\rm i}P_{+}{-}{\rm i}\epsilon)}{\Gamma\left(\widehat{\ell}{+}1{-}s{-}{\rm i}\epsilon\right)\Gamma\left(1{+}s{-}2{\rm i}P_{+}\right)\Gamma(\widehat{\ell}{+}1{+}2{\rm i}P_{+}{+}i\epsilon)}

(110)

In the static limit
\omega\to 0
one finds

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm Kerr}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}P_{+}(-)^{s-\ell-1}\frac{(\ell-s)!(\ell+s)!}{(2\ell)!(2\ell+1)^{2}}\prod_{n=1}\left(n^{2}+4P_{+}^{2}\right)

(111)

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}P_{+}(-)^{s-\ell-1}\frac{(\ell{-}s)!(\ell+s)!\Gamma(1{+}s{-}2{\rm i}P_{+})}{\ell!(2\ell+1)!\Gamma(1{-}s{+}2{\rm i}P_{+})}\prod_{n=1}\left(n^{2}+4P_{+}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(2\ell)!(\ell{-}s)!\Gamma(1{+}\ell{+}2{\rm i}P_{+})\Gamma(1{+}s{-}2{\rm i}P_{+})}{\ell!(\ell{+}s)!\Gamma\left(1{+}\ell{-}2{\rm i}P_{+}\right)\Gamma(1{-}s{+}2{\rm i}P_{+})}

7
Summary and Discussion

In this paper we derive a combinatorial formula for the PN expansion of the wave form produced by particles orbiting around Schwarzschild and Kerr BH’s. To this aim, we exploit techniques coming from Seiberg-Witten theory, localization and AGT duality. In this framework the wave form is written as the partition function of an
SU(2)^{2}
quiver gauge theory given as a double series with expansion parameters
accounting for, on the gravity side, to Post Newtonian and Post Minkowskian corrections. All log’s and trascendental numbers typically appearing in the PN expansions are resummed into exponential and Gammma functions. The results are in agreeement with those appeared in the literature obtained with other computational techniques and provide a compact formula for the wave form valid for arbitrary values of the orbital quantum number
\ell
.

The results show that, in the PN limit, the wave form is dominated by one of the two solutions of the Heun equation describing the gravitational perturbations at linear order. The second solution starts to contribute at the 5-th PN order beyond the quadrupole approximation. This mixing is encoded in the tidal Love function carrying the information of the boundary conditions specifying the gravitational object. We derive a formula for the tidal Love function for Kerr BH’s and Kerr like ECO’s.

It would be interesting to apply the techniques in this paper to the case of open orbits for which the Post Newtonian and the Post Minkoskian approximations are not directly related as in the case of circular orbits. This line of research would certainly contribute to the recent efforts to relate the results coming from scattering amplitudes, effective theories, worldline quantum field theories etc.
Each one of these techniques performs better than others in particular regions
providing complementary viewpoints which can be combined to improve our actual understanding.

CFT tools

(116)

The parameters
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots p_{n}
specify the intermediate momenta.
According to the AGT correspondence the conformal blocks of the Liouville theory can be related to the instanton partition function of a quiver gauge theory with gauge group
SU(2)^{n}
written as

\mathcal{F}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}..{}^{k_{n}}{}_{p_{n+1}}(z_{i})=\prod_{i=0}^{n}z_{i}^{\Delta_{p_{i}}{-}\Delta_{p_{i+1}}{-}\Delta_{k_{i}}}\prod_{\underset{i<j}{i,j=0}}^{2}\left(1{-}{z_{j}\over z_{i}}\right)^{(2k_{i}{-}bQ)(2k_{j}{+}bQ)\over 2b^{2}}\,Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}..{}^{k_{n}}{}_{p_{n+1}}

(117)

with
Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}..{}^{k_{n}}{}_{p_{n+1}}(q_{i})
given in (
38
),(
39
), (
40
).

(2,1)

(119)

and

p_{s}^{\alpha}=p_{s}+\frac{\alpha b^{2}}{2}

(120)

with
\alpha=\pm
.

A.2
The confluent limit

The degenerated five point conformal blocks

k_{0}
\infty
k_{\rm deg}
k_{2}
0
1
z
\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha}
p_{0}
z_{2}
\mathfrak{a}
p_{3}

(121)

satisfy a differential equation of the Heun type in the variable
z
. The confluent Heun equation is obtained by considering the
limit where two singularities collide at
z=0
. More precisely, we take
z_{2}
to zero,
k_{2}
,
p_{3}
to infinity, keeping finite the combinations

c=k_{2}+p_{3}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988ptx=z_{2}(k_{2}-p_{3}).

(122)

The confluent limits of the relevant four and five point conformal block obtained will be denoted as

(123)

In this limit the last contribution to the instanton partition function in (
39
) is replaced by

z_{2}^{|Y_{2}|}z^{\rm bifund}_{Y_{2},\emptyset}(\mathfrak{a},p_{3},-k_{2})\to x^{|Y_{2}|}z^{\rm hyp}_{Y_{2}}(\mathfrak{a},-c)

(124)

with

z^{\rm hyp}_{Y_{2}}(\mathfrak{a},-c)=\prod_{\alpha}\prod_{s\in Y_{\alpha}}\left[-E_{Y_{\alpha},\emptyset}(\alpha\mathfrak{a}{+}c,s){+}{\textstyle\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right]

(125)

On the other hand in the confluent limit

\left(1{-}{z_{2}\over z}\right)^{(2k_{\rm deg}{-}bQ)(2k_{2}{+}bQ)\over 2b^{2}}\to e^{{x\over 2z}}

(126)

We are interested in the
b\to 0
limit. It is easy to see that both
\Phi^{5}_{\alpha}
and
\Phi_{4}
are divergent but their ratio is finite

\Psi_{\alpha}(z)=\lim_{b\to 0}{\Phi^{5}_{\alpha}(z)\over\Phi_{4}}=e^{{x\over 2z}}\,z^{{1\over 2}+\alpha\mathfrak{a}}\,\lim_{b\to 0}\left(1{-}z\right)^{(2k_{0}{-}bQ)(2k_{\rm deg}{+}bQ)\over 2b^{2}}{Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}^{-\alpha}}{}^{k_{\rm deg}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}}{}_{c}(1,z,x)\over Z_{\rm inst}{}_{p_{0}}{}^{k_{0}}{}_{\mathfrak{a}}{}_{c}(1,x)}\,

(127)

The first few terms in the instanton expansion are given in (
43
). Similarly, the description of the solution in the near horizon zone can be obtained from
\Psi_{\alpha}(z)
with the use of braiding and fusion relations. Diagramatically

The three point function in (
129
) can be computed by expanding (
52
) in the limit
z/x\ll 1
, while the ratio of four point functions gives the instanton contribution. Finally comparing against (
3.3
) one finds (
63
).

Appendix B
The source term

In this appendix we collect some formulae needed for the evaluation of the source term in the Teukolsky equations. We refer the reader to
Mino:1997bx
and references therein, for details and explanations. We focus on spin
s=-2
perturbations corresponding to components

\Psi_{-2}=\rho^{-4}\psi_{4}=-\rho^{-4}C_{\mu\nu\sigma\rho}n^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\nu}n^{\sigma}\overline{m}^{\rho}

(130)

with
\rho=(r-ia\cos\theta)^{-1}
,
C_{\mu\nu\sigma\rho}
the Weyl tensor perturbation,

\displaystyle n^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\Sigma}}(r^{2}+a^{2},-\Delta_{r}(r),0,a)\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{m}^{\alpha}={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(r-{\rm i}a\cos\theta)}}\left(-{\rm i}a\sin\theta,0,1,-{{\rm i}\over\sin\theta}\right)

(131)

and
{\bf x}=(t,r,\theta,\phi)
. The corresponding stress energy tensor is
{\cal T}=2\rho^{-4}T_{4}
, that after being Fourier transformed produces the source term

T(r)=-{1\over\sqrt{2\pi}}\int d\chi d\phi dt{}_{-2}S_{\ell m}(\chi)e^{-im\varphi+i\omega t}{}_{-2}{\cal T}({\bf x})

(132)

where

{}_{-2}{\cal T}({\bf x})=2\rho^{3}L_{-1}[\rho^{-4}L_{0}(\rho^{-2}{\bar{\rho}}^{-1}T_{nn})]+\sqrt{2}\rho^{3}\Delta^{2}L_{-1}[\rho^{-4}{\bar{\rho}}^{2}J_{+}(\rho^{-2}{\bar{\rho}}^{-2}\Delta^{-1}T_{{\bar{m}}n})]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\rho^{3}\Delta^{2}J_{+}[\rho^{-4}J_{+}(\rho^{-2}{\bar{\rho}}T_{{\bar{m}}{\bar{m}}})]+\sqrt{2}\rho^{3}\Delta^{2}J_{+}[\rho^{-4}{\bar{\rho}}^{2}\Delta^{-1}L_{-1}(\rho^{-2}{\bar{\rho}}^{-2}T_{{\bar{m}}n})]

(133)

and

\displaystyle T_{nn}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}n^{\mu}n^{\nu}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptT_{n\overline{m}}=T_{\mu\nu}n^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\nu}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptT_{\overline{m}\overline{m}}=T_{\mu\nu}\overline{m}^{\mu}\overline{m}^{\nu}

\displaystyle L_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}+{m\over\sin\theta}-a\omega\sin\theta+s\cot\theta\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptL^{\dagger}_{s}=\partial_{\theta}-{m\over\sin\theta}+a\omega\sin\theta+s\cot\theta

\displaystyle J_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{r}+{iK/\Delta}

(134)

Given the source
T_{\ell m\omega}
, the solution of the inhomogenous equation can be written in the form (
15
-
26
) with the coefficients
A_{i}
given by
Mino:1997bx

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{-2\rho^{-2}{\bar{\rho}}^{-1}\over\Delta^{2}}C_{n\,n}L_{1}^{\dagger}\{\rho^{-4}L_{2}^{\dagger}(\rho^{3}S)\}-\rho^{-3}{\bar{\rho}}C_{{\bar{m}}\,{\bar{m}}}S\Bigl[-i\partial_{r}\Bigl({K\over\Delta}\Bigr)-{K^{2}\over\Delta^{2}}+2i\rho{K\over\Delta}\Bigr]

(135)

\displaystyle+{2\sqrt{2}\rho^{-3}\over\Delta}C_{{\bar{m}}\,n}\Bigl[\left(L_{2}^{\dagger}S\right)\Bigl({iK\over\Delta}+\rho+{\bar{\rho}}\Bigr)-a\sin\theta S{K\over\Delta}({\bar{\rho}}-\rho)\Bigr]

,

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{2\sqrt{2}\rho^{-3}\over\Delta}C_{{\bar{m}}\,n}[L_{2}^{\dagger}S+ia\sin\theta({\bar{\rho}}-\rho)S]-{2\rho^{-3}{\bar{\rho}}\over\sqrt{2\pi}}C_{{\bar{m}}\,{\bar{m}}}S\Bigl(i{K\over\Delta}+\rho\Bigr),

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\rho^{-3}{\bar{\rho}}C_{{\bar{m}}\,{\bar{m}}}S,

(136)

where
S={}_{-2}S_{\ell m}(\theta)
and

\displaystyle C_{n\,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mu\over 4\Sigma^{3}\dot{t}}\left[P_{t}(r^{2}+a^{2})+aP_{\phi}\right]^{2},

\displaystyle C_{{\bar{m}}\,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\mu\rho\over 2\sqrt{2}\Sigma^{2}\dot{t}}\left[P_{t}(r^{2}+a^{2})+aP_{\phi}\right]\left[i\sin\theta\Bigl(aP_{t}+{P_{\phi}\over\sin^{2}\theta}\Bigr)\right],

(138)

\displaystyle C_{{\bar{m}}\,{\bar{m}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\mu\rho^{2}\over 2\Sigma\dot{t}}\sin^{2}\theta\Bigl(aP_{t}+{P_{\phi}\over\sin^{2}\theta}\Bigr)^{2},

Appendix C
Quantum period
\mathfrak{a}

Here we give (
99
) up to
\epsilon^{4}
